<a id='question-111'></a>

### Question 111: Antiarrhythmic Therapy in Structural Heart Disease
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Class IC antiarrhythmics (e.g., flecainide) are contraindicated in patients with structural heart disease (e.g., prior MI, reduced LVEF) due to the increased risk of fatal proarrhythmia, as demonstrated by the CAST trial. (Class IC 反律动药 (例如氟卡尼) 在结构性心脏病 (例如既往 MI, LVEF 降低) 患者中是禁忌证，因为会增加致命性促心律失常的风险，正如 CAST 试验所证明的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 66-year-old male presents for follow-up cardiology evaluation. He has a history of an anterior myocardial infarction 5 years ago, resulting in a left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF) of 32%. He reports experiencing frequent premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) daily, which are asymptomatic but bothersome. An ECG confirms frequent PVCs originating from the left ventricle. His current medications include aspirin, metoprolol 50 mg twice daily, lisinopril 20 mg daily, and atorvastatin 80 mg daily. The cardiology resident is considering initiating flecainide to suppress the PVCs. Which of the following statements regarding the use of flecainide in this patient is most accurate?

(A) Flecainide is indicated for suppressing frequent PVCs in patients with reduced LVEF, as it effectively reduces the frequency of ventricular ectopy without significantly increasing mortality.
(B) Flecainide is contraindicated in this patient due to the increased risk of fatal ventricular proarrhythmia, as demonstrated by the Cardiac Arrhythmia Suppression Trial (CAST).
(C) Flecainide can be used cautiously in this patient if the PVCs are causing significant symptoms, provided the dose is carefully titrated and the patient is closely monitored for QT prolongation.
(D) Flecainide is safe in patients with prior myocardial infarction but should be avoided in those with dilated cardiomyopathy, as the risk of proarrhythmia is primarily associated with ischemic heart disease.
(E) Flecainide is a Class III antiarrhythmic agent that works by prolonging the action potential duration and refractory period, making it a suitable choice for suppressing ventricular ectopy in this patient.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 66-year-old male with a history of anterior MI 5 years ago. This indicates structural heart disease (scar tissue) and ischemic heart disease.
- **Cardiac Function:** LVEF of 32%. This signifies significant left ventricular systolic dysfunction, a major risk factor for arrhythmias and a key criterion for contraindication.
- **Presenting Symptom:** Frequent, asymptomatic PVCs. The indication for treatment is debatable (asymptomatic), but the question focuses on the appropriateness of a specific drug.
- **Current Medications:** Standard post-MI therapy (aspirin, beta-blocker, ACE inhibitor, statin). Beta-blocker (metoprolol) is already providing some antiarrhythmic effect.
- **Proposed Treatment:** Flecainide. This is a Class IC antiarrhythmic drug.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most accurate statement regarding the use of flecainide in this specific patient context.

The key clues are the history of MI, the reduced LVEF (structural/ischemic heart disease), and the proposed use of flecainide (a Class IC agent). This combination immediately raises the red flag of the CAST trial findings.

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
- **Flecainide:** A Class IC antiarrhythmic drug.
- **Mechanism of Action:** Primarily blocks fast sodium channels (INa) in the heart. This significantly slows conduction velocity (phase 0 depolarization) in atrial, ventricular, and His-Purkinje tissues. It has minimal effect on potassium channels (IKr, IKs) or calcium channels (ICaL).
- **Effect on Action Potential:** Prolongs the action potential duration (APD) and effective refractory period (ERP) primarily in atrial and ventricular myocytes, but *markedly slows conduction velocity*.
- **CAST Trial:** The Cardiac Arrhythmia Suppression Trial (CAST) was a landmark study published in 1989. It randomized patients with a history of MI and ventricular ectopy (PVCs or non-sustained VT) to receive either a Class IC agent (encainide or flecainide) or a Class IB agent (propafenone) or placebo.
- **CAST Findings:** The trial demonstrated that while Class IC agents effectively suppressed ventricular ectopy, they were associated with a statistically significant *increase* in the risk of sudden cardiac death and fatal ventricular proarrhythmia (e.g., ventricular fibrillation, polymorphic VT) compared to placebo. Class IB agents (propafenone) showed a trend towards increased mortality but did not reach statistical significance.
- **Mechanism of Proarrhythmia (Class IC):** In patients with structural heart disease (scar tissue from MI, dilated cardiomyopathy), re-entrant circuits often form around areas of slow conduction (e.g., scar). Class IC drugs, by profoundly slowing conduction velocity, can prolong the re-entrant circuit, making it more susceptible to degeneration into life-threatening ventricular fibrillation. The slowed conduction also increases the dispersion of repolarization, further increasing the risk of torsades de pointes-like arrhythmias.
- **Contraindication:** Based on the CAST trial, Class IC antiarrhythmics (flecainide, encainide, propafenone - though propafenone has some beta-blocking activity) are absolutely contraindicated in patients with structural heart disease (defined broadly as prior MI, LVEF < 35-40%, significant valvular heart disease, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, or heart failure).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** This statement directly contradicts the findings of the CAST trial. Flecainide *increases* mortality and the risk of fatal proarrhythmia in patients with structural heart disease, not decreases it. While it suppresses ectopy, this benefit is outweighed by the increased risk.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Flecainide is absolutely contraindicated, regardless of symptom severity, in patients with structural heart disease due to the high risk of fatal proarrhythmia. Symptom severity does not override this absolute contraindication. QT prolongation is primarily associated with Class IA and Class III agents, not Class IC.
- (D): **Incorrect.** The contraindication for Class IC agents applies to *any* structural heart disease, including both ischemic (prior MI) and non-ischemic (e.g., dilated cardiomyopathy, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy) causes. The risk is related to the presence of scar tissue or abnormal myocardial substrate that predisposes to re-entrant arrhythmias, which are exacerbated by the profound conduction slowing effect of Class IC drugs.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Flecainide is a Class IC antiarrhythmic, not Class III. Class III agents (e.g., amiodarone, sotalol, dofetilide) primarily block potassium channels, prolonging the action potential duration and refractory period. Class IC agents primarily block sodium channels, slowing conduction velocity.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The CAST trial established a critical contraindication for Class IC antiarrhythmics in patients with structural heart disease due to increased mortality from proarrhythmia. Remember this absolute contraindication for USMLE Step 1. Key differential: Class IB agents (e.g., lidocaine, mexiletine) are generally considered safer in ischemic heart disease (though not without risks) as they shorten the action potential duration and refractory period, potentially reducing the risk of re-entrant circuit prolongation. Class III agents are also generally contraindicated in structural heart disease due to proarrhythmic risks (Torsades de Pointes), although amiodarone is sometimes used cautiously.

<a id='question-112'></a>

### Question 112: Propafenone: Class IC Antiarrhythmic with Intrinsic Beta-Adrenergic Antagonism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Propafenone is a Class IC antiarrhythmic agent that blocks sodium channels and also possesses non-selective beta-adrenergic receptor blocking activity, leading to specific hemodynamic and clinical effects. (丙泊酮是一种IC类抗心律失常药物，它阻断钠通道，同时还具有非选择性β-肾上腺素受体阻滞活性，导致特定的血流动力学和临床影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of paroxysmal atrial fibrillation (PAF) and mild chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) presents to the emergency department complaining of palpitations and shortness of breath. His heart rate is 145 bpm, blood pressure is 110/70 mmHg, and respiratory rate is 24 breaths/min. He is currently taking lisinopril for hypertension. An ECG confirms atrial fibrillation with a rapid ventricular response. The physician decides to initiate intravenous propafenone for rate and rhythm control. Shortly after the infusion begins, the patient's heart rate decreases to 80 bpm, his blood pressure drops to 90/60 mmHg, and he develops wheezing. Arterial blood gas analysis reveals mild hypoxemia (PaO2 75 mmHg) and respiratory acidosis (PaCO2 50 mmHg).

Which of the following pharmacological mechanisms BEST explains the observed hemodynamic and respiratory changes following propafenone administration in this patient?

(A) Blockade of L-type calcium channels in cardiac myocytes, leading to decreased contractility and vasodilation.
(B) Inhibition of the Na+/K+-ATPase pump, causing intracellular sodium accumulation and impaired myocardial relaxation.
(C) Non-selective beta-adrenergic receptor antagonism, causing decreased heart rate, reduced cardiac contractility, and bronchoconstriction.
(D) Blockade of potassium channels (IKr), prolonging the cardiac action potential duration and increasing the risk of torsades de pointes.
(E) Inhibition of phosphodiesterase-3, leading to increased intracellular cAMP, vasodilation, and positive inotropy.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with PAF and mild COPD. This history is crucial because COPD makes him susceptible to bronchoconstriction from beta-blockers. PAF necessitates rate/rhythm control.
- **Presentation:** Palpitations, shortness of breath, tachycardia (HR 145 bpm), hypotension (BP 110/70 mmHg initially), tachypnea (RR 24), ECG confirms AFib with RVR.
- **Intervention:** IV propafenone initiated for rate/rhythm control.
- **Adverse Effects:** Bradycardia (HR decreased to 80 bpm), hypotension (BP dropped to 90/60 mmHg), wheezing (bronchoconstriction), hypoxemia (PaO2 75 mmHg), respiratory acidosis (PaCO2 50 mmHg).
- **Question:** Mechanism explaining these specific adverse effects (bradycardia, hypotension, bronchoconstriction).

The key adverse effects are bradycardia, hypotension, and bronchoconstriction. Propafenone is known as a Class IC antiarrhythmic. Class IC drugs primarily block sodium channels. However, propafenone has a unique characteristic: it also possesses non-selective beta-adrenergic receptor blocking activity (similar to propranolol). This beta-blocking effect explains the observed bradycardia (decreased heart rate), hypotension (reduced cardiac output due to decreased contractility and potentially vasodilation), and bronchoconstriction (especially relevant in a patient with COPD).

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
- **Propafenone Mechanism:** Propafenone is a Class IC antiarrhythmic drug. Its primary mechanism is blocking fast sodium channels (INa) in cardiac myocytes, slowing phase 0 depolarization and conduction velocity, particularly in ischemic tissue. This contributes to its antiarrhythmic effect.
- **Unique Feature:** Propafenone also exhibits non-selective beta-adrenergic receptor antagonism (blocking both β1 and β2 receptors). This is due to its structural similarity to propranolol.
- **Beta-1 Blockade Effects:** Blocking β1 receptors in the heart leads to:
    - Decreased heart rate (negative chronotropy).
    - Decreased myocardial contractility (negative inotropy).
    - Decreased AV nodal conduction.
    - Reduced renin release.
    These effects contribute to the observed bradycardia and hypotension.
- **Beta-2 Blockade Effects:** Blocking β2 receptors in the lungs and peripheral vasculature leads to:
    - Bronchoconstriction (especially problematic in patients with asthma or COPD).
    - Peripheral vasoconstriction (though the overall effect on BP can be complex due to decreased cardiac output).
    - Inhibition of glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis.
    The bronchoconstriction explains the wheezing and worsening respiratory status (hypoxemia, respiratory acidosis) in this patient with COPD.
- **Overall Effect:** The combination of sodium channel blockade (antiarrhythmic) and beta-adrenergic blockade (bradycardia, hypotension, bronchoconstriction) characterizes propafenone's profile. The question specifically asks for the mechanism explaining the *adverse* effects observed (bradycardia, hypotension, bronchoconstriction).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Blockade of L-type calcium channels is characteristic of Class IV antiarrhythmics (e.g., verapamil, diltiazem). These drugs cause bradycardia, hypotension, and AV nodal blockade but do *not* typically cause bronchoconstriction. This mechanism is incorrect for propafenone.
- (B): Inhibition of the Na+/K+-ATPase pump is the mechanism of action for cardiac glycosides (e.g., digoxin). Digoxin increases intracellular calcium, leading to positive inotropy, but it also slows AV conduction and can cause bradycardia. It does not cause bronchoconstriction and its primary effect is not sodium channel blockade. This mechanism is incorrect for propafenone.
- (D): Blockade of potassium channels (specifically IKr) is characteristic of Class III antiarrhythmics (e.g., amiodarone, sotalol, dofetilide). These drugs prolong the action potential duration and QT interval, increasing the risk of torsades de pointes. While some Class III drugs might have beta-blocking effects (like sotalol), the primary mechanism described here (potassium channel blockade) does not explain the bronchoconstriction seen with propafenone. This mechanism is incorrect for propafenone.
- (E): Inhibition of phosphodiesterase-3 (PDE3) is the mechanism of action for drugs like milrinone and inamrinone. PDE3 inhibition increases intracellular cAMP, leading to vasodilation (especially pulmonary) and positive inotropy. This would cause tachycardia and hypotension (due to vasodilation) but would *not* cause bradycardia or bronchoconstriction. This mechanism is incorrect for propafenone and describes the opposite hemodynamic effects.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Propafenone is a Class IC antiarrhythmic with unique non-selective beta-blocking properties. This dual mechanism explains its efficacy in rate and rhythm control but also accounts for adverse effects like bradycardia, hypotension, and bronchoconstriction, particularly in patients with underlying respiratory disease. Remember to differentiate propafenone from other Class IC agents (like flecainide, which lack significant beta-blocking activity) and from Class IV (calcium channel blockers) and Class III (potassium channel blockers) antiarrhythmics. Also, contrast its effects with Class II agents (beta-blockers) which primarily cause bradycardia, hypotension, and bronchoconstriction but lack the potent sodium channel blocking effect needed for rhythm control in AFib.

### Question 113: Beta-Blockers and AV Nodal Conduction
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Class II antiarrhythmics (beta-blockers) decrease heart rate and slow AV nodal conduction by blocking beta-1 adrenergic receptors in pacemaker cells, reducing the slope of phase 4 depolarization. (II类抗心律失常药（β受体阻滞剂）通过阻断心房肌细胞中的β1肾上腺素受体来降低心率和减慢房室结传导，从而减少4期去极化斜率。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and stable angina pectoris presents to the emergency department complaining of palpitations and shortness of breath. His vital signs show a heart rate of 165 bpm, blood pressure of 130/80 mmHg, respiratory rate of 22 breaths/min, and oxygen saturation of 96% on room air. An ECG reveals irregularly irregular rhythm with no discernible P waves and a rapid ventricular response, consistent with atrial fibrillation (AFib). The patient is hemodynamically stable. The physician decides to initiate intravenous metoprolol for rate control. Which of the following is the primary mechanism by which metoprolol achieves rate control in this patient?

(A) Blocking L-type calcium channels in the sinoatrial (SA) node, decreasing the influx of calcium ions during phase 0 depolarization and slowing SA nodal firing rate.
(B) Increasing the activity of the sympathetic nervous system, leading to enhanced calcium influx through T-type channels in the atrioventricular (AV) node, thereby increasing AV nodal conduction velocity.
(C) Blocking beta-1 adrenergic receptors in the AV node, decreasing the slope of phase 4 depolarization by inhibiting the Gs-cAMP-PKA pathway, thereby slowing AV nodal conduction.
(D) Blocking potassium channels in the ventricular myocytes, prolonging the action potential duration and the effective refractory period, which indirectly slows the ventricular rate by increasing the refractory period of the AV node.
(E) Enhancing the inward rectifier potassium current (IK1) in the SA node, hyperpolarizing the cell membrane potential and increasing the threshold for firing, thus decreasing the SA nodal firing rate.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with hypertension, angina, presenting with palpitations, shortness of breath, and rapid heart rate (165 bpm).
- **ECG Findings:** Irregularly irregular rhythm, no P waves, rapid ventricular response – diagnostic of atrial fibrillation (AFib).
- **Clinical Goal:** Rate control in hemodynamically stable AFib.
- **Intervention:** Intravenous metoprolol is administered.
- **Question:** Mechanism of metoprolol's rate control effect.
- **Key Clue:** Metoprolol is a beta-blocker (Class II antiarrhythmic). The question asks for its mechanism in the context of AFib rate control, specifically focusing on the AV node. AFib involves rapid atrial activity, and the AV node acts as a gatekeeper, determining how many atrial impulses reach the ventricles. Rate control aims to slow the ventricular response by slowing conduction through the AV node.

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
- **Metoprolol:** A selective beta-1 adrenergic receptor antagonist.
- **Target Tissue:** Primarily the sinoatrial (SA) node and atrioventricular (AV) node, which are rich in beta-1 receptors.
- **Mechanism in Pacemaker Cells (SA/AV Nodes):**
    - **Normal Physiology:** Catecholamines (epinephrine, norepinephrine) bind to beta-1 receptors on SA and AV nodal cells. These receptors are coupled to Gs proteins.
    - **Gs Protein Activation:** Activation of Gs stimulates adenylyl cyclase.
    - **cAMP Production:** Adenylyl cyclase converts ATP to cyclic AMP (cAMP).
    - **PKA Activation:** cAMP activates protein kinase A (PKA).
    - **Ion Channel Modulation:** PKA phosphorylates various ion channels, including:
        - **L-type Ca2+ channels:** Phosphorylation increases Ca2+ influx during phase 0 (depolarization) in SA/AV nodes, increasing firing rate.
        - **T-type Ca2+ channels:** Phosphorylation increases Ca2+ influx during phase 0 in SA/AV nodes, increasing firing rate.
        - **Funny current (If) channels (HCN channels):** Phosphorylation increases Na+ influx during phase 4 (spontaneous diastolic depolarization) in SA/AV nodes, increasing the slope of phase 4 depolarization and thus increasing the automaticity (firing rate).
        - **Potassium channels (e.g., K+ACh):** Phosphorylation decreases K+ efflux, contributing to depolarization.
    - **Beta-Blocker Action:** Metoprolol blocks beta-1 receptors, preventing catecholamine binding.
    - **Consequences of Beta-Blockade:**
        - Inhibition of Gs activation.
        - Decreased cAMP production.
        - Decreased PKA activation.
        - Reduced phosphorylation of target ion channels.
        - **Crucially, decreased phosphorylation of HCN channels (If) and other channels contributing to phase 4 depolarization.** This leads to a *decrease* in the slope of phase 4 depolarization in SA and AV nodal cells.
        - A slower slope of phase 4 means it takes longer for the membrane potential to reach the threshold for firing an action potential.
        - This results in a *decreased* firing rate of the SA node (negative chronotropy) and a *slowing* of conduction velocity through the AV node (negative dromotropy).
        - In AFib, slowing AV nodal conduction limits the number of rapid atrial impulses that pass through to the ventricles, thus controlling the ventricular rate.
- **Therefore, metoprolol slows AV nodal conduction primarily by decreasing the slope of phase 4 depolarization via beta-1 receptor blockade.**

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Blocking L-type calcium channels in the SA node:** This describes the mechanism of non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers (e.g., verapamil, diltiazem). While they slow SA nodal firing rate and AV nodal conduction, metoprolol's primary mechanism is beta-1 blockade, not direct calcium channel blockade. This is incorrect.
- **(B) Increasing the activity of the sympathetic nervous system:** Metoprolol *blocks* the effects of the sympathetic nervous system (specifically catecholamines acting on beta-1 receptors). Increasing sympathetic activity would *increase* heart rate and AV conduction, the opposite of metoprolol's effect. This is incorrect.
- **(D) Blocking potassium channels in the ventricular myocytes:** Blocking potassium channels (e.g., Class III antiarrhythmics like amiodarone, sotalol) prolongs the action potential duration and refractory period, primarily in ventricular myocytes. While this can indirectly affect AV nodal conduction by prolonging its refractory period, it is not the *primary* mechanism by which metoprolol controls ventricular rate in AFib. Metoprolol's main effect is on the SA and AV nodes via beta-1 blockade. This is incorrect.
- **(E) Enhancing the inward rectifier potassium current (IK1) in the SA node:** Enhancing IK1 (an outward potassium current) would lead to *hyperpolarization* and *stabilization* of the membrane potential, making it harder to reach the threshold for firing, thus decreasing the SA nodal firing rate. While this would decrease heart rate, it is not the mechanism of beta-blockers. Beta-blockers decrease the *slope* of phase 4 depolarization by reducing If and other inward currents, not by enhancing outward potassium currents like IK1. This is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Beta-blockers (Class II antiarrhythmics) control ventricular rate in atrial fibrillation primarily by slowing AV nodal conduction through blockade of beta-1 receptors, which decreases the slope of phase 4 depolarization in AV nodal cells.
- **Differentials:** Compare the mechanism of beta-blockers (beta-1 blockade affecting phase 4 slope) with calcium channel blockers (verapamil/diltiazem - L-type Ca2+ channel blockade affecting phase 0 and phase 4) and Class III agents (potassium channel blockade affecting repolarization/refractory period). Understand the specific electrophysiological targets (SA node, AV node, ventricular myocytes) and the phase of the action potential affected by each drug class.

### Question 114: Class III Antiarrhythmics
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Class III antiarrhythmics (e.g., Sotalol, Dofetilide) block potassium channels (IKr), prolonging repolarization (QT interval). They exhibit reverse use-dependence, meaning their effect is *stronger* at *slower* heart rates, increasing the risk of Torsades de Pointes. (第三类抗心律失常药阻断钾通道，延长复极，表现为QT间期延长。它们表现出逆向依赖性，即其作用在心率*降低*时*增强*，从而增加尖端扭转型室速的风险。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF) and paroxysmal atrial fibrillation (PAF) is admitted to the hospital for management of symptomatic bradycardia. He was recently started on oral dofetilide 250 mcg twice daily for rate control of his PAF. His baseline ECG showed a normal PR interval, QRS duration, and QT interval (420 ms). His current medications include lisinopril, metoprolol succinate 50 mg daily, and digoxin 0.125 mg daily. Vital signs show a heart rate of 45 bpm, blood pressure 110/70 mmHg, respiratory rate 16/min, and oxygen saturation 98% on room air. Physical examination reveals a regular but slow heart rhythm with no murmurs, rubs, or gallops. A 12-lead ECG shows a significantly prolonged QT interval (580 ms) and a prominent U wave. Serum potassium is 4.2 mEq/L. The patient reports feeling lightheaded and dizzy.

Which of the following best explains the observed ECG changes and the increased risk of arrhythmia in this patient?

(A) Dofetilide blocks sodium channels, leading to QRS prolongation and increased risk of ventricular tachycardia due to slowed conduction.
(B) Dofetilide blocks calcium channels, prolonging the plateau phase (Phase 2) of the cardiac action potential and increasing the risk of AV block.
(C) Dofetilide blocks the rapid delayed rectifier potassium current (IKr), prolonging Phase 3 repolarization and the QT interval, with this effect being enhanced at slower heart rates (reverse use-dependence).
(D) Dofetilide blocks the L-type calcium current, leading to increased intracellular calcium concentration and enhanced myocardial contractility, which can trigger arrhythmias.
(E) Dofetilide blocks the inward rectifier potassium current (IK1), prolonging Phase 4 repolarization and increasing the risk of atrial fibrillation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** Elderly male with HFrEF and PAF, treated with dofetilide.
- **Presenting Symptom:** Symptomatic bradycardia (HR 45 bpm).
- **Medications:** Dofetilide (Class III antiarrhythmic), metoprolol (beta-blocker), digoxin (positive inotrope, slows AV conduction). Metoprolol and digoxin can contribute to bradycardia.
- **ECG Findings:** Significantly prolonged QT interval (580 ms) and prominent U wave.
- **Question:** Mechanism explaining the ECG changes (QT prolongation) and increased arrhythmia risk.
- **Key Clue:** The patient is bradycardic (HR 45 bpm) and has a markedly prolonged QT interval while taking dofetilide. This points towards a drug effect that is exacerbated by slow heart rates.

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
- **Dofetilide:** A Class III antiarrhythmic agent.
- **Primary Mechanism:** Dofetilide selectively blocks the rapid component of the delayed rectifier potassium current (IKr). This current is responsible for repolarization (Phase 3) of the cardiac action potential in atrial and ventricular myocytes.
- **Effect on Action Potential:** Blocking IKr prolongs Phase 3 repolarization, leading to an increase in the action potential duration (APD) and the effective refractory period (ERP).
- **ECG Manifestation:** Prolongation of the QT interval on the ECG reflects the increased APD. A QT interval > 500 ms is generally considered significantly prolonged and increases the risk of Torsades de Pointes (TdP).
- **Reverse Use-Dependence:** Unlike Class I antiarrhythmics (e.g., lidocaine, flecainide) which exhibit use-dependence (blockade is stronger at faster heart rates), Class III agents like dofetilide and sotalol exhibit reverse use-dependence. This means their potassium channel blocking effect is *stronger* when the heart rate is *slower*.
- **Physiological Explanation for Reverse Use-Dependence:** At slower heart rates, the time spent in Phase 3 repolarization is longer. This allows more time for the drug to bind to and block the IKr channels, leading to a greater prolongation of the action potential duration and QT interval.
- **Clinical Consequence:** The combination of QT prolongation and reverse use-dependence significantly increases the risk of early afterdepolarizations (EADs), which can trigger polymorphic ventricular tachycardia known as Torsades de Pointes, especially in the setting of bradycardia. The patient's bradycardia (HR 45 bpm) likely potentiated the QT-prolonging effect of dofetilide, leading to the observed ECG changes (QT 580 ms) and increased risk of TdP.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** Dofetilide is a Class III agent, not a Class I agent. Class I agents block sodium channels (e.g., flecainide, lidocaine), which primarily affects Phase 0 depolarization, leading to QRS prolongation and slowed conduction velocity. While sodium channel blockade can cause arrhythmias, it's not the primary mechanism of dofetilide, nor does it explain the QT prolongation seen here.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Dofetilide does not significantly block calcium channels. Calcium channel blockers (e.g., verapamil, diltiazem) primarily affect Phase 2 (plateau) repolarization and AV nodal conduction. While they can cause bradycardia and AV block, they do not typically cause significant QT prolongation like dofetilide.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Dofetilide's primary action is potassium channel blockade, not L-type calcium channel blockade. L-type calcium channel blockers (like verapamil/diltiazem) affect Phase 2 and AV conduction. While increased intracellular calcium can trigger arrhythmias, this is not the mechanism of dofetilide.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Dofetilide primarily blocks IKr, which affects Phase 3 repolarization. The inward rectifier potassium current (IK1) is primarily responsible for Phase 4 repolarization (resting membrane potential) in nodal tissue and atrial myocytes. Blocking IK1 (e.g., with cesium) would prolong Phase 4 and potentially increase automaticity or trigger AF, but this is not the mechanism of dofetilide, nor does it explain the QT prolongation.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Class III antiarrhythmics (like Dofetilide, Sotalol, Amiodarone) prolong the QT interval by blocking IKr potassium channels. They exhibit reverse use-dependence, meaning their effect is enhanced at slower heart rates, increasing the risk of Torsades de Pointes.
- **Differentials:** Class I agents block Na+ channels (QRS prolongation, use-dependence). Class II agents block beta-adrenergic receptors. Class IV agents block L-type Ca2+ channels (AV node block). Class V agents (e.g., digoxin) have complex mechanisms including Na+/K+ ATPase inhibition and vagal stimulation. Remember the mnemonic "Rhythm is King" (Class I & III) for ventricular arrhythmias, and "Rate is Queen" (Class II & IV) for atrial arrhythmias/rate control.

### Question 115: Amiodarone: Multi-Channel Electrophysiological Spectrum
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Amiodarone's unique multi-channel electrophysiological effects (Class I, II, III, IV) and its paradoxical low risk of Torsades de Pointes despite QT prolongation. (阿米odarone独特的四类电生理效应 (I, II, III, IV) 及其 QT 间期延长但 Torsades de Pointes 风险低的悖论)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of atrial fibrillation (AFib), hypertension, and heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF) is admitted to the cardiology ward. He has been experiencing paroxysmal AFib for 5 years, managed initially with metoprolol, but recently experienced breakthrough episodes despite uptitration to the maximum tolerated dose. His current medications include metoprolol succinate 200 mg daily, lisinopril 20 mg daily, furosemide 40 mg daily, and digoxin 0.125 mg daily. An echocardiogram shows an LVEF of 35%. An ECG reveals a rate-controlled AFib with a ventricular rate of 75 bpm. Given his HFrEF and inadequate rate control with a beta-blocker, the cardiologist decides to initiate amiodarone therapy. During a subsequent electrophysiology study (EPS) evaluating the effects of amiodarone on myocardial tissue, the following observations are made:
1.  Prolongation of the action potential duration (APD) in ventricular myocytes.
2.  Slowing of conduction velocity through the AV node.
3.  Decreased amplitude of the L-type calcium current (ICa,L).
4.  Reduced peak sodium current (INa) during the inactivated state.
5.  Blockade of both early (IKr) and delayed (IKs) repolarizing potassium currents.

Which of the following statements BEST describes the comprehensive electrophysiological mechanism of action of amiodarone, explaining its efficacy and relatively low risk of Torsades de Pointes?

(A) Amiodarone primarily blocks L-type calcium channels (Class IV effect) and enhances sodium channel inactivation (Class I effect), leading to significant QT prolongation but minimal effect on potassium channels, thus preventing Torsades de Pointes.
(B) Amiodarone predominantly blocks inactivated sodium channels (Class I effect) and beta-adrenergic receptors (Class II effect), causing moderate QT prolongation and a slightly increased risk of Torsades de Pointes compared to other Class I agents.
(C) Amiodarone exhibits balanced effects across multiple ion channels, including blockade of sodium channels (Class I), beta-adrenergic receptors (Class II), potassium channels (Class III), and calcium channels (Class IV), which contributes to its efficacy in various arrhythmias and paradoxically reduces the risk of Torsades de Pointes despite QT prolongation.
(D) Amiodarone acts primarily as a Class III antiarrhythmic by selectively blocking the rapid component of the delayed rectifier potassium current (IKr), leading to significant QT prolongation and a high risk of Torsades de Pointes, especially in patients with underlying structural heart disease.
(E) Amiodarone's main mechanism involves blocking alpha-adrenergic receptors (Class II effect) and enhancing calcium influx (opposite of Class IV), which slows AV nodal conduction but has minimal impact on ventricular repolarization or QT interval.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with AFib, HFrEF, hypertension. This context highlights the need for an antiarrhythmic effective in structural heart disease and potentially with less proarrhythmic risk.
*   **Current Therapy:** Metoprolol (beta-blocker), lisinopril (ACEi), furosemide (loop diuretic), digoxin. Inadequate rate control despite beta-blocker.
*   **New Therapy:** Amiodarone initiated.
*   **Electrophysiology Study Findings:**
    *   Prolongation of APD (ventricular myocytes) -> K+ channel blockade (Class III).
    *   Slowing of AV nodal conduction -> Ca2+ channel blockade (Class IV) and/or beta-blockade (Class II).
    *   Decreased ICa,L amplitude -> Ca2+ channel blockade (Class IV).
    *   Reduced peak INa (inactivated state) -> Na+ channel blockade (Class I).
    *   Blockade of IKr and IKs -> K+ channel blockade (Class III).
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the BEST description of amiodarone's comprehensive electrophysiological mechanism and its relation to QT prolongation and Torsades de Pointes risk.

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
Amiodarone is unique among antiarrhythmics because it possesses properties of all four Vaughan-Williams classes:
*   **Class I (Sodium Channel Blockade):** Amiodarone blocks inactivated sodium channels, similar to Class IA agents, but with slower kinetics. This effect contributes to slowing conduction velocity in ischemic tissue and potentially suppressing ventricular ectopy.
*   **Class II (Beta- and Alpha-Adrenergic Blockade):** Amiodarone non-competitively blocks both alpha- and beta-adrenergic receptors. Beta-blockade contributes to slowing the heart rate and AV nodal conduction, while alpha-blockade may cause vasodilation.
*   **Class III (Potassium Channel Blockade):** This is considered amiodarone's primary mechanism. It blocks multiple potassium channels, most notably the rapid component of the delayed rectifier potassium current (IKr) and the slow component (IKs). Blocking these channels prolongs the action potential duration (APD) and the effective refractory period (ERP) in atrial and ventricular myocytes, increasing the refractory period and making the tissue less excitable. This effect is responsible for the significant QT interval prolongation seen on the ECG.
*   **Class IV (Calcium Channel Blockade):** Amiodarone blocks L-type calcium channels, similar to Class IV agents like verapamil and diltiazem. This effect contributes to slowing conduction through the AV node and potentially reducing myocardial oxygen demand.

The combination of these effects leads to broad-spectrum antiarrhythmic activity. The paradoxical low risk of Torsades de Pointes (<1%) despite significant QT prolongation is attributed to the *balanced* blockade of multiple ion channels. While Class III agents typically prolong repolarization and increase the risk of early afterdepolarizations (EADs) which can trigger Torsades, amiodarone's Class I (Na+ channel blockade) and Class IV (Ca2+ channel blockade) effects may stabilize the membrane potential and reduce the likelihood of EADs. Furthermore, its beta-blocking effect may also play a role. The net effect is that while QT prolongation is prominent, the proarrhythmic risk associated with Torsades is remarkably low compared to other potent Class III agents like sotalol or dofetilide.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. Amiodarone blocks *multiple* potassium channels (IKr and IKs), not just minimal effect. It also has significant Class I, II, and IV effects. This option downplays the Class III effect and incorrectly states minimal effect on K+ channels.
- (B): Incorrect. While amiodarone does have Class I (Na+ channel blockade) and Class II (beta-blockade) effects, this option fails to mention its crucial Class III (K+ channel blockade) and Class IV (Ca2+ channel blockade) properties. It also incorrectly states a "slightly increased risk" compared to other Class I agents; amiodarone's overall proarrhythmic risk, including Torsades, is low despite QT prolongation.
- (D): Incorrect. Amiodarone is *not* primarily a Class III agent in the sense of *selectively* blocking IKr like some other drugs. Its Class III effect is significant but occurs alongside substantial Class I, II, and IV effects. Furthermore, while it does cause significant QT prolongation and has a *low* risk of Torsades, this option incorrectly states a *high* risk.
- (E): Incorrect. Amiodarone *does* block beta-adrenergic receptors (Class II), not alpha. It also blocks Ca2+ channels (Class IV), which *decreases* calcium influx, not enhances it. This option misrepresents both Class II and Class IV effects and incorrectly states minimal impact on ventricular repolarization.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Amiodarone is a unique antiarrhythmic drug with multi-channel blocking properties (Class I, II, III, IV). This broad spectrum explains its efficacy against various arrhythmias but also necessitates careful monitoring. Despite causing significant QT prolongation (Class III effect), its balanced action across multiple channels results in a paradoxically low risk of Torsades de Pointes compared to other Class III agents. Key differentials include sotalol (primarily Class III, higher Torsades risk), lidocaine (Class IB), verapamil (Class IV), and beta-blockers (Class II). Understanding amiodarone's complex pharmacology is crucial for USMLE Step 1.

<a id='question-116'></a>

### Question 116: Amiodarone Pharmacokinetics
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Amiodarone's unique pharmacokinetic profile, specifically its massive volume of distribution (Vd) due to high lipophilicity and extensive tissue sequestration, and its extremely long elimination half-life (t1/2), leading to prolonged effects after discontinuation. (阿米odarone独特的药代动力学特征，特别是由于高脂溶性和广泛的组织分布导致的巨大分布容积 (Vd) 以及极其长的消除半衰期 (t1/2)，导致停药后效应持续时间长。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of atrial fibrillation and heart failure (LVEF 35%) has been stable on oral amiodarone 200 mg daily for 6 months to maintain sinus rhythm. He presents to the emergency department complaining of worsening fatigue, nausea, and visual disturbances (halos around lights) over the past 2 weeks. His vital signs are stable: BP 125/75 mmHg, HR 60 bpm (regular), RR 16/min, SpO2 98% on room air. Physical examination reveals mild scleral icterus and peripheral edema. Laboratory tests show elevated liver enzymes (AST 180 U/L, ALT 150 U/L) and slightly elevated creatinine (1.4 mg/dL). An EKG shows no significant changes from baseline. The patient reports strict adherence to his medication regimen. He was recently started on warfarin for stroke prevention due to his atrial fibrillation, and his INR is currently 2.8. Given his symptoms and lab findings, the physician decides to discontinue amiodarone. The patient asks when he can expect his symptoms related to amiodarone to resolve.

(A) Within 1-2 days, as the drug is primarily eliminated renally and its effects are short-lived.
(B) Within 1-2 weeks, corresponding to approximately 4-5 half-lives of the drug.
(C) Within 2-3 months, reflecting the time required for the drug to be eliminated from its extensive tissue stores.
(D) Within 6-12 months, as the drug accumulates irreversibly in certain tissues like the retina and liver.
(E) Immediately, as the symptoms are likely due to warfarin toxicity, not amiodarone.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with atrial fibrillation and heart failure, on chronic amiodarone.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Fatigue, nausea, visual disturbances (halos), scleral icterus, peripheral edema. These symptoms, along with elevated liver enzymes and creatinine, are suggestive of amiodarone toxicity (hepatotoxicity, nephrotoxicity, corneal microdeposits causing halos).
- **Medication Adherence:** Patient reports strict adherence.
- **Recent Change:** Started on warfarin, INR is therapeutic (2.8), making warfarin toxicity less likely as the primary cause of *all* symptoms (especially visual halos and scleral icterus).
- **Decision:** Amiodarone is discontinued.
- **Question:** When will symptoms resolve after discontinuation?
- **Key Clue:** The question asks about the *duration* of effects *after discontinuation*. This points directly to the drug's elimination kinetics, specifically its half-life and tissue distribution.

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
- **Amiodarone Pharmacokinetics:** Amiodarone is highly lipophilic (logP ~ 4.5). This property leads to a very large volume of distribution (Vd ≈ 60 L/kg, or ~4000 L in a 70kg person). It extensively distributes into and binds to adipose tissue, liver, lungs, spleen, and other tissues.
- **Half-Life:** Due to its extensive tissue sequestration and slow release back into the plasma, amiodarone has an extremely long elimination half-life, typically ranging from 40 to 60 days (average ~50 days). Some sources state it can be even longer, up to several months.
- **Active Metabolites:** Amiodarone is metabolized primarily by CYP3A4 and CYP2C8 to several active metabolites (e.g., desethylamiodarone, deisopropylamiodarone), which also have long half-lives and contribute to the overall pharmacological and toxicological effects.
- **Clinical Implication:** Because of the long half-life and extensive tissue storage, amiodarone's effects (both therapeutic and toxic) persist for a considerable time after discontinuation. It takes approximately 5 half-lives for a drug to reach steady state, and similarly, it takes about 5 half-lives for the drug concentration to decrease significantly after discontinuation.
- **Calculation:** With a half-life of ~50 days, 5 half-lives would be 5 * 50 days = 250 days (approximately 8 months). However, the question asks when symptoms will *resolve*, not when the plasma concentration reaches a negligible level. Many effects, especially those related to tissue accumulation (like corneal deposits or thyroid dysfunction), can take several months to fully resolve. The options provided reflect different time scales related to drug elimination. Option (C) suggests 2-3 months, which aligns with the time needed for significant elimination from tissue stores and resolution of many toxic effects, considering the long half-life. While complete elimination might take longer, clinically significant effects often diminish substantially within this timeframe.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** Amiodarone is primarily eliminated via hepatic metabolism and biliary excretion, with some renal excretion of metabolites. Its effects are *not* short-lived due to its extremely long half-life. This option confuses amiodarone with drugs that are rapidly eliminated renally.
- (B): **Incorrect.** 1-2 weeks corresponds to roughly 0.2-0.4 half-lives. At this point, the drug concentration in plasma and tissues would still be very high, and effects (both therapeutic and toxic) would persist. This option underestimates the duration of action significantly.
- (D): **Incorrect.** While amiodarone does accumulate in certain tissues (cornea, liver, lungs, skin), this accumulation is generally considered reversible over time, although some effects like corneal deposits might take longer than 2-3 months to fully resolve. The term "irreversibly" is too strong and inaccurate for the general persistence of effects. Furthermore, the timeframe of 6-12 months is longer than typically required for significant symptom resolution, although complete resolution of all tissue effects might take that long. Option (C) is a better estimate for the resolution of *most* symptoms.
- (E): **Incorrect.** While warfarin can cause nausea and fatigue, it does not typically cause visual halos or scleral icterus. The patient's symptoms (especially visual halos and elevated liver enzymes) are classic signs of amiodarone toxicity. Although the patient is on warfarin, the constellation of symptoms points strongly towards amiodarone as the cause.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Amiodarone has an exceptionally long half-life (~50 days) and extensive tissue distribution, leading to prolonged pharmacological and toxicological effects that persist for months after discontinuation.
- **Differentials:** Compare amiodarone's kinetics to other antiarrhythmics (e.g., lidocaine - short half-life, low Vd; sotalol - moderate half-life, moderate Vd) or drugs with long half-lives (e.g., digoxin - long half-life, moderate Vd, primarily renal elimination; warfarin - long half-life, high protein binding, hepatic metabolism). Amiodarone is unique in its combination of extreme lipophilicity, massive Vd, and very long half-life.

<a id='question-117'></a>

### Question 117: Amiodarone Toxicity Spectrum
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Amiodarone is an antiarrhythmic drug with a wide range of potential toxicities affecting multiple organ systems, including the lungs, thyroid, eyes, skin, liver, and heart. Understanding the specific mechanisms and clinical presentations of these toxicities is crucial. (阿米odarone是一种抗心律失常药物，具有广泛的潜在毒性，会影响多个器官系统，包括肺、甲状腺、眼睛、皮肤、肝脏和心脏。了解这些毒性的具体机制和临床表现至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of atrial fibrillation and heart failure (LVEF 35%) has been taking oral amiodarone 200 mg daily for the past 18 months. He initially presented with palpitations and shortness of breath, which improved significantly with amiodarone therapy. He has been compliant with his medication and regular follow-up appointments. He denies smoking history. Over the last 3 months, he has developed a persistent, non-productive cough and progressive shortness of breath, particularly with exertion. His physical examination reveals bibasilar crackles on lung auscultation. A chest X-ray shows bilateral interstitial reticular opacities. Pulmonary function tests (PFTs) demonstrate a restrictive pattern with a significantly reduced diffusing capacity for carbon monoxide (DLCO). Thyroid function tests reveal elevated TSH and low free T4. An ophthalmologic examination shows whorl-like corneal deposits. Which of the following adverse effects is MOST likely responsible for the patient's worsening respiratory symptoms and radiographic findings?

(A) Amiodarone-induced hyperthyroidism leading to increased cardiac output and pulmonary edema.
(B) Amiodarone-induced hypothyroidism leading to pleural effusions and decreased respiratory drive.
(C) Amiodarone-induced pulmonary fibrosis resulting from direct alveolar toxicity and inflammation.
(D) Amiodarone-induced optic neuropathy causing blurred vision and central respiratory depression.
(E) Amiodarone-induced hepatotoxicity leading to ascites and diaphragmatic dysfunction.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male, atrial fibrillation, heart failure (LVEF 35%), taking amiodarone for 18 months. This establishes the context of long-term amiodarone use in a patient with underlying cardiac conditions.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Persistent non-productive cough, progressive dyspnea on exertion over 3 months. These are classic symptoms of interstitial lung disease.
- **Physical Exam:** Bibasilar crackles. Suggestive of interstitial lung disease or fluid overload.
- **Radiographic Findings:** Chest X-ray shows bilateral interstitial reticular opacities. This pattern is characteristic of pulmonary fibrosis or interstitial pneumonitis.
- **Pulmonary Function Tests (PFTs):** Restrictive pattern (decreased lung volumes) with significantly reduced DLCO. This confirms impaired gas exchange and lung parenchymal disease, consistent with fibrosis.
- **Thyroid Function Tests:** Elevated TSH, low free T4. Indicates amiodarone-induced hypothyroidism.
- **Ophthalmologic Examination:** Whorl-like corneal deposits (cornea verticillata). A common, usually benign, side effect of amiodarone.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely cause of the worsening respiratory symptoms and radiographic findings (fibrosis).

The combination of chronic cough, progressive dyspnea, bibasilar crackles, interstitial reticular opacities on CXR, restrictive PFTs with low DLCO, and long-term amiodarone use strongly points towards amiodarone-induced pulmonary toxicity, specifically pulmonary fibrosis. The other findings (hypothyroidism, corneal deposits) are also known side effects of amiodarone but do not explain the respiratory presentation.

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
Amiodarone is a class III antiarrhythmic agent structurally related to thyroxine. Its adverse effects are numerous and affect nearly every organ system. Pulmonary toxicity is one of the most serious and potentially fatal side effects.
- **Mechanism of Pulmonary Toxicity:** The exact mechanism is not fully elucidated but is thought to involve direct toxicity to alveolar epithelial cells and pulmonary fibroblasts, leading to inflammation, fibrosis, and impaired gas exchange. Amiodarone or its metabolites (like desethylamiodarone) accumulate in the lungs, causing oxidative stress, lipid peroxidation, and inflammation. This results in chronic interstitial pneumonitis, which can progress to pulmonary fibrosis.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Typically insidious onset (months to years after starting therapy), characterized by non-productive cough, progressive dyspnea, fatigue, and weight loss.
- **Radiographic Findings:** Chest X-ray often shows bilateral interstitial reticular or nodular opacities, predominantly in the lower lobes. High-resolution CT (HRCT) may show ground-glass opacities, honeycombing, and traction bronchiectasis.
- **PFTs:** Typically show a restrictive pattern (decreased FVC, TLC) with a disproportionately reduced DLCO, reflecting impaired gas exchange across the thickened alveolar-capillary membrane.
- **Management:** Requires immediate discontinuation of amiodarone. Treatment often involves corticosteroids, but prognosis is variable, and the condition can be fatal.
- **Thyroid Effects:** Amiodarone contains a large amount of iodine and inhibits the peripheral conversion of T4 to T3 (by blocking type 1 and type 2 deiodinases). This can lead to hypothyroidism (Wolff-Chaikoff effect) or hyperthyroidism (Jod-Basedow phenomenon, especially in iodine-deficient areas, or destructive thyroiditis). The patient's elevated TSH and low free T4 indicate hypothyroidism.
- **Ocular Effects:** Corneal microdeposits (cornea verticillata) are very common (>90%) but usually asymptomatic and reversible upon drug discontinuation. Optic neuropathy is less common but can cause vision loss.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Amiodarone-induced hyperthyroidism can occur, but it typically presents with symptoms like palpitations, anxiety, weight loss, and heat intolerance. While severe hyperthyroidism can exacerbate heart failure and potentially lead to pulmonary edema, the patient's symptoms (chronic cough, progressive dyspnea), PFTs (restrictive pattern, low DLCO), and thyroid function tests (hypothyroidism, not hyperthyroidism) are inconsistent with this mechanism being the primary cause of the respiratory findings. The patient has hypothyroidism (high TSH, low T4).
- (B): Amiodarone-induced hypothyroidism is present in this patient (high TSH, low T4). Hypothyroidism can cause fatigue and potentially pleural effusions (myxedema), but it does not typically cause the interstitial lung changes, restrictive PFTs with low DLCO, or progressive dyspnea characteristic of pulmonary fibrosis. Pleural effusions are usually small and not the primary respiratory manifestation.
- (D): Amiodarone can cause optic neuropathy, leading to blurred vision, visual field defects, or even blindness. It does not cause central respiratory depression. The patient's primary symptoms are respiratory, not visual, and the lung findings are not explained by optic neuropathy.
- (E): Amiodarone can cause hepatotoxicity, ranging from elevated liver enzymes to cirrhosis. Severe liver disease can lead to ascites, which can restrict diaphragmatic movement and cause dyspnea. However, the patient's presentation (chronic cough, interstitial pattern on CXR, restrictive PFTs with low DLCO) is much more characteristic of pulmonary fibrosis than hepatotoxicity causing diaphragmatic dysfunction. There is no mention of jaundice, abdominal swelling, or abnormal liver function tests in the vignette.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Amiodarone has a wide spectrum of toxicities. Pulmonary fibrosis is a serious, potentially fatal adverse effect characterized by insidious onset of cough and dyspnea, interstitial infiltrates on imaging, and restrictive PFTs with reduced DLCO. It requires immediate drug discontinuation. Other common toxicities include thyroid dysfunction (hypo- or hyperthyroidism), corneal microdeposits, skin discoloration, and hepatotoxicity. Differentiating amiodarone-induced pulmonary fibrosis from other causes of interstitial lung disease is crucial. Key differentials include idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis, drug-induced pneumonitis from other agents (e.g., bleomycin, methotrexate), hypersensitivity pneumonitis, and infections. Always consider amiodarone toxicity in patients on this drug presenting with respiratory symptoms.

<a id='question-118'></a>

### Question 118: Dronedarone Contraindication in Heart Failure
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Dronedarone is contraindicated in patients with symptomatic heart failure (NYHA Class II-IV) or permanent atrial fibrillation due to increased risk of cardiovascular death, stroke, and heart failure hospitalization. (Dronedarone 在有症状的心力衰竭 (NYHA II-IV 级) 或永久性房颤患者中是禁忌证，因为会增加心血管死亡、卒中和心力衰竭住院的风险。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 72-year-old male with a history of hypertension, hyperlipidemia, and coronary artery disease (status post-CABG 5 years ago) presents to the cardiology clinic complaining of palpitations and shortness of breath with exertion. An ECG reveals atrial fibrillation with a rapid ventricular response (RVR). His echocardiogram shows a left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF) of 35% and evidence of moderate mitral regurgitation. He is currently taking lisinopril, atorvastatin, aspirin, and metoprolol succinate. His New York Heart Association (NYHA) functional class is determined to be Class III based on his symptoms. The cardiologist is considering initiating an antiarrhythmic medication to control his atrial fibrillation and improve his symptoms. Which of the following antiarrhythmic agents is absolutely contraindicated in this patient?

(A) Amiodarone
(B) Sotalol
(C) Dofetilide
(D) Dronedarone
(E) Propafenone

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 72-year-old male with significant cardiovascular comorbidities (HTN, HLD, CAD s/p CABG).
- **Presenting Complaint:** Palpitations and dyspnea on exertion.
- **Diagnosis:** Atrial fibrillation with RVR.
- **Echocardiogram Findings:** LVEF 35% (reduced systolic function, indicating heart failure), moderate mitral regurgitation.
- **NYHA Class:** III (Significant limitation of physical activity; comfortable at rest, but ordinary physical activity results in marked discomfort, palpitation, dyspnea, or headache). This signifies symptomatic heart failure.
- **Current Medications:** Lisinopril (ACE inhibitor for HTN/HF), Atorvastatin (statin for HLD), Aspirin (antiplatelet for CAD), Metoprolol succinate (beta-blocker for HTN/AF/HF).
- **Question Stem:** Which antiarrhythmic agent is absolutely contraindicated?

The key clues are the patient's reduced LVEF (35%) and NYHA Class III heart failure. This indicates symptomatic heart failure. The question asks for an *absolutely contraindicated* antiarrhythmic agent.

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
- **Dronedarone:** A synthetic benzofuran derivative structurally related to amiodarone but lacks iodine atoms and is less lipophilic. It has Class III (potassium channel blockade) and Class II (beta-adrenergic blockade) antiarrhythmic properties, with weaker Class I activity compared to amiodarone. It has a shorter half-life (~24 hours).
- **ANDROMEDA Trial:** This pivotal trial demonstrated that dronedarone significantly increased the risk of cardiovascular death, stroke, and hospitalization for heart failure in patients with permanent atrial fibrillation and symptomatic heart failure (NYHA Class II-IV) or those recently decompensated.
- **Mechanism of Contraindication:** The exact mechanism for the increased risk in heart failure is not fully elucidated but may involve its effects on cardiac contractility, hemodynamics, or interactions with other heart failure medications. The key takeaway is the established clinical evidence from large trials showing harm in this specific patient population.
- **Amiodarone:** Also a Class III agent (prominent K+ channel blockade) with Class I, II, and IV properties. It is *not* contraindicated in heart failure; in fact, it is often used in patients with heart failure and atrial fibrillation, although it has significant long-term toxicities (thyroid, pulmonary, liver, ocular).
- **Sotalol:** A Class III agent (K+ channel blockade) with non-selective beta-blockade (Class II). It can be used in patients with heart failure but requires careful monitoring due to the risk of QT prolongation and torsades de pointes, especially in patients with reduced LVEF. It is not absolutely contraindicated.
- **Dofetilide:** A pure Class III agent (K+ channel blockade). It is effective for cardioversion and maintenance of sinus rhythm in atrial fibrillation. It carries a risk of QT prolongation and torsades de pointes, requiring inpatient initiation and monitoring, especially in patients with renal impairment or reduced LVEF. It is not absolutely contraindicated in heart failure, but caution is needed.
- **Propafenone:** A Class IC agent (sodium channel blockade). It is generally contraindicated in patients with structural heart disease (e.g., CAD, LV dysfunction) due to the risk of proarrhythmia and increased mortality in this setting (CAST trial). However, the specific contraindication related to symptomatic heart failure is most strongly associated with dronedarone based on the ANDROMEDA trial results.

The patient has symptomatic heart failure (NYHA Class III, LVEF 35%). Dronedarone is specifically contraindicated in this population due to the ANDROMEDA trial findings.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Amiodarone: Incorrect. Amiodarone is *not* contraindicated in heart failure and is often used in this setting despite its toxicity profile. It lacks the specific contraindication demonstrated for dronedarone in the ANDROMEDA trial.
- (B) Sotalol: Incorrect. Sotalol is a Class III antiarrhythmic with beta-blocking activity. While it requires caution in patients with reduced LVEF due to QT prolongation risk, it is not absolutely contraindicated in symptomatic heart failure.
- (C) Dofetilide: Incorrect. Dofetilide is a pure Class III agent. Like sotalol, it requires caution and specific monitoring protocols in patients with reduced LVEF or renal impairment, but it is not absolutely contraindicated in symptomatic heart failure.
- (E) Propafenone: Incorrect. Propafenone is a Class IC agent generally contraindicated in patients with structural heart disease (like CAD or LV dysfunction) due to proarrhythmic risk shown in the CAST trial. While this patient has structural heart disease (CAD, reduced LVEF), the *specific* contraindication highlighted by major clinical trials (ANDROMEDA) applies most directly to dronedarone in the context of symptomatic heart failure. Dronedarone's contraindication is more directly linked to worsening heart failure outcomes than propafenone's general contraindication in structural heart disease.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Dronedarone is absolutely contraindicated in patients with symptomatic heart failure (NYHA Class II-IV) or permanent atrial fibrillation due to increased risk of cardiovascular death, stroke, and heart failure hospitalization, as demonstrated in the ANDROMEDA trial. This is a critical distinction from amiodarone, which is often used in heart failure despite its toxicities. Remember the ANDROMEDA trial warning for dronedarone. Key differentials include amiodarone (used in HF), sotalol (caution in HF), dofetilide (caution in HF), and propafenone (contraindicated in structural heart disease).

<a id='question-119'></a>

### Question 119: Sotalol and Renal Insufficiency
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Sotalol is a Class III antiarrhythmic agent with non-selective beta-blocking activity (Class II) that is primarily eliminated renally. Its use is contraindicated or requires significant dose adjustment in patients with renal impairment due to the risk of accumulation, excessive QT prolongation, and Torsades de Pointes. (索他洛尔是一种III类抗心律失常药物，具有非选择性β受体阻滞活性（II类），主要通过肾脏排泄。由于积累、过度QT间期延长和尖端扭转型室速的风险，它在肾功能不全患者中是禁忌或需要显著剂量调整的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of symptomatic atrial fibrillation and chronic kidney disease (CKD) stage 4 (estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] 25 mL/min/1.73 m²) is being evaluated for rate control. He has been experiencing palpitations and shortness of breath. His current medications include lisinopril, metoprolol succinate 50 mg daily, and warfarin. His vital signs are: blood pressure 130/80 mmHg, heart rate 95 bpm (irregularly irregular), respiratory rate 16 breaths/min, and oxygen saturation 98% on room air. Physical examination reveals a normal S1 and S2, no murmurs, and mild peripheral edema. An ECG shows atrial fibrillation with a ventricular rate of 95 bpm and a QTc interval of 440 ms. The cardiologist decides to initiate sotalol for rate control, starting with a dose of 80 mg twice daily.

Which of the following is the MOST significant concern regarding the initiation of sotalol in this patient?

(A) Increased risk of hepatotoxicity due to hepatic metabolism of the d-isomer.
(B) Potential for severe hypotension due to potent alpha-1 adrenergic blockade.
(C) Risk of excessive QT prolongation and Torsades de Pointes due to impaired renal clearance and drug accumulation.
(D) Development of bronchospasm due to non-selective beta-2 adrenergic blockade.
(E) Increased risk of thromboembolic events due to potent inhibition of platelet aggregation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with atrial fibrillation and CKD stage 4 (eGFR 25 mL/min/1.73 m²). This immediately flags renal impairment as a critical factor.
- **Medications:** Lisinopril (ACE inhibitor), metoprolol succinate (beta-1 selective blocker), warfarin (anticoagulant). Metoprolol is already providing some rate control, but sotalol is being considered.
- **Vitals/ECG:** HR 95 bpm (AFib), QTc 440 ms (normal range generally <450 ms for males). The baseline QTc is normal, but sotalol is known to prolong it.
- **Drug Choice:** Sotalol 80 mg BID. This is the key drug in question.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST significant concern.

The core issue is the interaction between sotalol's pharmacology and the patient's severe renal impairment (CKD stage 4). Sotalol is primarily renally excreted. Impaired renal function leads to decreased clearance, drug accumulation, increased plasma concentrations, and consequently, an amplified risk of its adverse effects, particularly QT prolongation and Torsades de Pointes.

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
- **Sotalol Pharmacology:** Sotalol is a racemic mixture. The l-isomer is a non-selective beta-adrenergic antagonist (Class II antiarrhythmic effect), blocking beta-1 and beta-2 receptors. The d-isomer and the l-isomer both block the delayed rectifier potassium channel IKr (Class III antiarrhythmic effect). Blocking IKr prolongs the action potential duration and the effective refractory period, leading to QT interval prolongation on the ECG.
- **Renal Elimination:** Sotalol is >80% excreted unchanged by the kidneys via glomerular filtration and tubular secretion. It undergoes minimal hepatic metabolism.
- **Renal Impairment:** In patients with renal insufficiency (like this patient with CKD stage 4, eGFR 25), sotalol clearance is significantly reduced. This leads to accumulation of the drug in the plasma.
- **Consequences of Accumulation:** Increased plasma concentrations of sotalol potentiate both its beta-blocking (Class II) and potassium channel blocking (Class III) effects. The most feared consequence of excessive Class III effect is marked QT prolongation, which increases the risk of developing Torsades de Pointes, a potentially fatal polymorphic ventricular tachycardia. The beta-blocking effect can also contribute to bradycardia, especially in combination with other rate-controlling agents like metoprolol.
- **Clinical Management:** Due to this risk, initiation of sotalol in patients with renal impairment requires extreme caution, often starting with very low doses, careful monitoring of renal function, and continuous ECG monitoring (typically inpatient for at least 3 days) to detect QT prolongation or arrhythmias. The FDA mandates this monitoring.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** Sotalol undergoes minimal hepatic metabolism. The d-isomer does contribute to Class III activity, but its metabolism is not the primary concern, and hepatotoxicity is not a major side effect associated with sotalol. The primary elimination route is renal.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Sotalol's beta-blocking activity is non-selective (blocks both beta-1 and beta-2), but it does *not* have significant alpha-1 adrenergic blocking activity. Hypotension can occur due to bradycardia or negative inotropy from beta-blockade, but alpha-1 blockade is not the mechanism. Prazosin, terazosin, doxazosin are alpha-1 blockers.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Sotalol is a *non-selective* beta-blocker, meaning it blocks beta-2 receptors. Beta-2 blockade in the lungs can cause bronchospasm, particularly in patients with asthma or COPD. However, while this is a potential side effect, the *most significant* concern in a patient with severe renal impairment is the risk of QT prolongation and Torsades de Pointes due to drug accumulation. Bronchospasm is a general contraindication or caution for non-selective beta-blockers, not specific to renal failure.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Sotalol does not have significant antiplatelet activity. Increased risk of thromboembolic events is associated with conditions like atrial fibrillation itself or with drugs that affect coagulation (like warfarin, which this patient is already on). Antiplatelet drugs like aspirin or clopidogrel inhibit platelet aggregation.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Sotalol is primarily eliminated by the kidneys. Renal impairment significantly increases the risk of sotalol accumulation, leading to excessive QT prolongation and potentially fatal Torsades de Pointes. Always consider renal function before prescribing sotalol.
- **Differentials:** Compare sotalol's renal elimination and QT prolongation risk with other antiarrhythmics. Amiodarone has complex pharmacokinetics (hepatic metabolism, renal excretion) and also prolongs QT but has less accumulation risk in renal failure compared to sotalol. Dofetilide is also a Class III agent with renal excretion, requiring dose adjustment in renal impairment, but sotalol's beta-blocking effects add another layer of concern. Procainamide is primarily hepatically metabolized. Beta-blockers like metoprolol are primarily hepatically metabolized.

<a id='question-120'></a>

### Question 120: Atrial Fibrillation Cardioversion and QT Interval Prolongation
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating the mechanisms of action and specific adverse effect profiles of Class III antiarrhythmic drugs (ibutilide vs. dofetilide) used for atrial fibrillation cardioversion, particularly regarding QT interval prolongation and risk of torsades de pointes (TdP). (区分用于心房颤动的心律复律的 III 类抗心律失常药物的作用机制和特定不良反应谱，特别是关于 QT 间期延长和尖端扭转型室速 (TdP) 的风险。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of hypertension and heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF, LVEF 35%) presents to the emergency department with palpitations and shortness of breath. An ECG reveals rapid, irregularly irregular atrial fibrillation (AF) of recent onset (within 48 hours). His blood pressure is 110/70 mmHg, heart rate is 140 bpm, and respiratory rate is 24/min. He is currently taking lisinopril, metoprolol succinate, and furosemide. The physician decides to attempt chemical cardioversion. After reviewing the patient's chart, the cardiologist notes the patient has a baseline corrected QT interval (QTc) of 440 ms. The cardiologist chooses to administer an intravenous antiarrhythmic agent known for its rapid onset and efficacy in converting recent-onset AF, but also carries a significant risk of QT prolongation and TdP, necessitating continuous ECG monitoring for at least 4 hours post-infusion. Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the potential for this agent to induce TdP in this patient?

(A) Blockade of the rapid component of the delayed rectifier potassium current (IKr) and inhibition of the L-type calcium channel.
(B) Blockade of the slow component of the delayed rectifier potassium current (IKs) and inhibition of the inward rectifier potassium current (IK1).
(C) Blockade of the rapid component of the delayed rectifier potassium current (IKr) and promotion of a slow inward sodium current (INa-slow).
(D) Blockade of the L-type calcium channel and inhibition of the inward rectifier potassium current (IK1).
(E) Blockade of the slow component of the delayed rectifier potassium current (IKs) and inhibition of the L-type calcium channel.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male, HFrEF (LVEF 35%), hypertension, recent-onset AF (<48h). This profile suggests a patient at risk for AF and potentially vulnerable to drug-induced arrhythmias. HFrEF is relevant as some antiarrhythmics are contraindicated or require caution in this setting.
- **Presentation:** Palpitations, SOB, rapid AF (140 bpm).
- **Baseline QTc:** 440 ms. This is borderline prolonged (normal <450 ms for males), increasing the risk of further QT prolongation and TdP with QT-prolonging drugs.
- **Treatment Choice:** Intravenous agent for rapid chemical cardioversion of recent-onset AF. This points towards ibutilide.
- **Adverse Effect Profile:** Significant risk of QT prolongation and TdP, requiring continuous ECG monitoring for >= 4 hours. This is a hallmark characteristic of ibutilide.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism BEST explaining the potential for TdP induction by this agent. This requires knowledge of the specific electrophysiological effects of ibutilide.

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
- **Ibutilide:** A Class III antiarrhythmic agent. Class III agents primarily block potassium channels, prolonging repolarization (QT interval).
- **Mechanism of Ibutilide:** Ibutilide is a pure Class III agent, meaning its primary action is blocking potassium channels. Specifically, it blocks the *rapid component of the delayed rectifier potassium current (IKr)*, which is encoded by the HERG gene (KCNH2). Blocking IKr prolongs the action potential duration (APD) and the effective refractory period (ERP) in atrial and ventricular myocytes.
- **Unique Feature of Ibutilide:** Unlike many other Class III agents (like amiodarone or sotalol), ibutilide *also* blocks the *slow inward sodium current (INa-slow)*. This INa-slow current is responsible for the plateau phase (phase 2) of the ventricular action potential. Blocking INa-slow shortens the APD and ERP in ventricular myocytes, counteracting the effect of IKr blockade to some extent. However, the net effect, especially in the context of pre-existing QT prolongation or underlying cardiac disease, is still significant QT prolongation.
- **Torsades de Pointes (TdP):** TdP is a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia associated with QT interval prolongation. It occurs when delayed repolarization (prolonged QT) leads to early afterdepolarizations (EADs), which can trigger arrhythmias. The risk is higher with baseline QT prolongation, electrolyte abnormalities (hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia), bradycardia, and certain drugs.
- **Why Ibutilide causes TdP:** Its potent blockade of IKr leads to significant QT prolongation. The additional blockade of INa-slow, while potentially shortening the APD slightly, does not eliminate the risk of TdP, especially in vulnerable patients. The combination of potent IKr blockade and the potential for further QT prolongation makes ibutilide carry a significant risk (4-8%) of TdP, necessitating close monitoring.
- **Comparing Ibutilide and Dofetilide:** Dofetilide is another pure Class III agent, but it *selectively* blocks IKr without significant effects on other channels like INa-slow. While dofetilide also prolongs the QT interval and carries a risk of TdP, the risk profile is generally considered lower than ibutilide, especially when initiated carefully in an inpatient setting with QTc titration. The question specifically describes a drug with a high risk requiring 4 hours of monitoring, characteristic of ibutilide.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Blockade of IKr is correct for ibutilide, but the addition of L-type calcium channel blockade is incorrect. Ibutilide does not significantly block L-type calcium channels. L-type calcium channel blockers are Class IV antiarrhythmics (e.g., verapamil, diltiazem).
- (B): Blockade of IKs is characteristic of Class III agents like amiodarone, but not the primary mechanism of ibutilide. Blockade of IK1 is characteristic of Class II agents (beta-blockers) or Class IV agents (non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers). Ibutilide's primary target is IKr.
- (D): Blockade of the L-type calcium channel is incorrect for ibutilide. Inhibition of IK1 is also incorrect for ibutilide. This combination describes Class IV antiarrhythmics.
- (E): Blockade of IKs is incorrect for ibutilide's primary mechanism. Blockade of the L-type calcium channel is also incorrect. This combination is not characteristic of ibutilide.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Ibutilide is a Class III antiarrhythmic that blocks IKr and INa-slow, leading to QT prolongation and a significant risk of TdP, requiring close monitoring post-infusion. Dofetilide is another Class III agent that selectively blocks IKr, also causing QT prolongation but generally considered to have a lower TdP risk when used cautiously.
- **Differentials:** Compare ibutilide (IV, rapid onset, high TdP risk, blocks IKr + INa-slow) with dofetilide (oral, slower onset, lower TdP risk, blocks IKr only), amiodarone (blocks IKr, IKs, INa, Ca2+ channels, complex profile), sotalol (blocks IKr, beta-blocker, proarrhythmic risk). Understand the specific ion channel targets (IKr, IKs, INa, Ca2+) and their contribution to QT prolongation and proarrhythmia.
